- [ ] **fd12** (adult) Myth: Thawing on the counter is safe. Fix: Never thaw food on the counter. Germs multiply in the parts that warm up. Source: [CDC: Preventing Food Poisoning](https://www.cdc.gov/food-safety/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **fd13** (both) Myth: Warm water thaws food fast and safely. Fix: Thaw in the fridge at 41°F or less, under cold running water, in a microwave right before cooking, or as part of cooking. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd14** (both) Myth: Hot running water is the best way. Fix: Yes, fully under running drinking water at 70°F or below. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd15** (both) Myth: Chicken is done at 145°F like beef. Fix: All poultry must reach 165°F (74°C) inside. Source: [FoodSafety.gov: Safe Minimum Internal Temperatures](https://www.foodsafety.gov/food-safety-charts/safe-minimum-internal-temperatures)
- [ ] **fd16** (both) Myth: Pink is fine since steak can be pink. Fix: Ground meat and sausage must reach 160°F (71°C). Source: [FoodSafety.gov: Safe Minimum Internal Temperatures](https://www.foodsafety.gov/food-safety-charts/safe-minimum-internal-temperatures)
- [ ] **fd17** (both) Myth: All meat must reach 165°F. Fix: Steaks, roasts and chops: 145°F, then rest 3 minutes. Source: [FoodSafety.gov: Safe Minimum Internal Temperatures](https://www.foodsafety.gov/food-safety-charts/safe-minimum-internal-temperatures)
- [ ] **fd18** (adult) Myth: Just warm it until it steams a bit. Fix: Leftovers must reach 165°F (74°C). Source: [FoodSafety.gov: Safe Minimum Internal Temperatures](https://www.foodsafety.gov/food-safety-charts/safe-minimum-internal-temperatures)
- [ ] **fd19** (adult) Myth: Wild game is safe rare. Fix: Rabbit and venison, wild or farm-raised: 160°F (71°C). Source: [FoodSafety.gov: Safe Minimum Internal Temperatures](https://www.foodsafety.gov/food-safety-charts/safe-minimum-internal-temperatures)
- [ ] **fd20** (both) Myth: Clear juice means it is done. Fix: The only way to tell is a food thermometer. Color and feel can fool you. Source: [CDC: Preventing Food Poisoning](https://www.cdc.gov/food-safety/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **fd21** (ya) Myth: Standing time is only so it's not too hot. Fix: Letting it sit lets cold spots soak up heat and cook more fully. Source: [CDC: Preventing Food Poisoning](https://www.cdc.gov/food-safety/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **fd22** (both) Myth: Serve it right out of the microwave. Fix: Stir or turn it, cover it, and let it stand covered for 2 minutes after reaching 165°F. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd23** (both) Myth: Clams are done after a quick steam. Fix: Cook clams, oysters and mussels until the shells open. Source: [FoodSafety.gov: Safe Minimum Internal Temperatures](https://www.foodsafety.gov/food-safety-charts/safe-minimum-internal-temperatures)
- [ ] **fd24** (adult) Myth: A runny yolk is always safe. Fix: Cook eggs until both the yolk and white are firm. Source: [FoodSafety.gov: Safe Minimum Internal Temperatures](https://www.foodsafety.gov/food-safety-charts/safe-minimum-internal-temperatures)
- [ ] **fd25** (both) Myth: Rinsing chicken makes it cleaner. Fix: Raw chicken does not need washing. Washing spreads germs to the sink and counter. Source: [CDC: Preventing Food Poisoning](https://www.cdc.gov/food-safety/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **fd26** (both) Myth: A quick wipe makes the board clean. Fix: Use one board for raw meat and a separate one for foods that won't be cooked. Source: [CDC: Preventing Food Poisoning](https://www.cdc.gov/food-safety/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **fd27** (both) Myth: Only hot water gets your hands clean. Fix: Wash for at least 20 seconds with soap and warm or cold water. Source: [CDC: Preventing Food Poisoning](https://www.cdc.gov/food-safety/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **fd28** (ya) Myth: Eggless cookie dough is safe raw. Fix: Raw dough or batter made with uncooked flour is risky. Source: [CDC: Preventing Food Poisoning](https://www.cdc.gov/food-safety/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **fd29** (adult) Myth: Sprouts are a safe health food. Fix: Raw or undercooked sprouts are a known food safety risk. Source: [CDC: Preventing Food Poisoning](https://www.cdc.gov/food-safety/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **fd30** (adult) Myth: Fresh, raw cider is always safe. Fix: Unpasteurized milk, juice, cider and soft cheese can carry germs. Source: [CDC: Preventing Food Poisoning](https://www.cdc.gov/food-safety/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **fd31** (both) Myth: A fast rinse meets the rule. Fix: Food workers must clean hands and exposed arms for at least 20 seconds. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd32** (both) Myth: Palms are all that matter. Fix: Pay special attention to the soil under the fingernails. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd33** (both) Myth: Just rinse off the knife. Fix: Wash hands when switching between raw food and ready-to-eat food. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd34** (both) Myth: New gloves alone are enough. Fix: Wash hands after coughing, sneezing or using a tissue. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd35** (both) Myth: Clean-looking hands are fine. Fix: Workers may not touch ready-to-eat food with bare hands; use utensils or gloves. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd36** (both) Myth: A quick snack at the prep table is fine. Fix: Eat, drink or use tobacco only in set-aside areas away from food. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd37** (both) Myth: Tasting from the stirring spoon is fine. Fix: Use a clean utensil for the sample and taste it away from the food. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd38** (both) Myth: Only long hair needs covering. Fix: Food workers wear hair restraints so hair and sweat stay off food. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd39** (both) Myth: Gloves make a sick cook safe. Fix: A food worker who is vomiting or has diarrhea must be kept out of work. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd40** (both) Myth: Yellow skin doesn't matter in a kitchen. Fix: A worker with jaundice in the last 7 days is kept out unless a doctor clears her. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd41** (both) Myth: Pick out the spot and serve the rest. Fix: Food contaminated by body fluids must be thrown out. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd42** (both) Myth: Cool to the touch is fine. Fix: Cold food that needs temperature control must be 41°F or below when received. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd43** (both) Myth: Refrozen ice is no big deal. Fix: Food must arrive with no signs of earlier temperature abuse. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd44** (both) Myth: Warm is fine if it's on time. Fix: Cooked food received hot must be at 135°F (57°C) or above. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd45** (both) Myth: Ice is ice; it is clean. Fix: Ice used to cool the outside of food or cans may not be used as food. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd46** (both) Myth: Where raw meat goes doesn't matter. Fix: Keep raw animal foods apart from ready-to-eat and cooked foods. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd47** (both) Myth: Raw meats can all mix since they get cooked. Fix: Separate types of raw animal foods, like beef, fish and poultry, from each other. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd48** (both) Myth: Cold food keeps for weeks. Fix: Food prepared ahead and held cold may be kept up to 7 days, counting the prep day as day 1. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd49** (both) Myth: Food can sit out a whole shift. Fix: Time alone may control food for up to 4 hours, and the food must be marked. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd50** (both) Myth: Keep the rag handy in a pocket. Fix: Hold wiping cloths in sanitizer solution between uses and launder them daily. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd51** (both) Myth: One rag works for all surfaces. Fix: Cloths used on raw animal food surfaces must be kept separate. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd52** (both) Myth: Any meat thermometer works for thin patties. Fix: A small-diameter probe is needed to measure thin foods like patties and fillets. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd53** (both) Myth: A shared towel is fine. Fix: Each sink needs soap and drying: paper towels or a hand dryer. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd54** (both) Myth: Anywhere is fine for a few minutes. Fix: Store in-use utensils in the food with handles above the food and container. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd55** (both) Myth: Sanitizer kills every germ. Fix: Wash hands with soap and water. Sanitizer alone does not work well on norovirus. Source: [CDC: Preventing Norovirus](https://www.cdc.gov/norovirus/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **fd56** (both) Myth: Once you feel better, you are safe. Fix: Wait at least 2 days (48 hours) after symptoms stop. Source: [CDC: Preventing Norovirus](https://www.cdc.gov/norovirus/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **fd57** (adult) Myth: It's gone once you feel okay. Fix: Norovirus can stay in your poop for 2 weeks or more. Source: [CDC: Preventing Norovirus](https://www.cdc.gov/norovirus/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **fd58** (adult) Myth: You can't spread it before you feel sick. Fix: Norovirus can be in vomit or poop even before you feel sick. Source: [CDC: Preventing Norovirus](https://www.cdc.gov/norovirus/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **fd59** (adult) Myth: A quick steam makes shellfish safe. Fix: Quick steaming does not heat food enough. Cook shellfish to at least 145°F. Source: [CDC: Preventing Norovirus](https://www.cdc.gov/norovirus/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **fd60** (ya) Myth: Norovirus makes food look or smell off. Fix: Food with norovirus may look, smell and taste normal. Source: [CDC: Preventing Norovirus](https://www.cdc.gov/norovirus/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **fd61** (adult) Myth: Wipe bleach right off. Fix: Leave bleach cleaner on the area for at least 5 minutes. Source: [CDC: Preventing Norovirus](https://www.cdc.gov/norovirus/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **fd62** (adult) Myth: Shake them out first. Fix: Handle soiled items carefully without shaking them. Source: [CDC: Preventing Norovirus](https://www.cdc.gov/norovirus/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **fd63** (ya) Myth: Fruit is always safe. Fix: Cut melon is a known food safety risk. Keep it cold. Source: [CDC: Preventing Food Poisoning](https://www.cdc.gov/food-safety/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **fd64** (ya) Myth: Shrimp is done as soon as it floats. Fix: Cook shrimp, lobster, crab and scallops until pearly or white, and opaque. Source: [FoodSafety.gov: Safe Minimum Internal Temperatures](https://www.foodsafety.gov/food-safety-charts/safe-minimum-internal-temperatures)
- [ ] **fd65** (ya) Myth: Egg dishes are done when they look set. Fix: Egg dishes like quiche or frittata must reach 160°F (71°C). Source: [FoodSafety.gov: Safe Minimum Internal Temperatures](https://www.foodsafety.gov/food-safety-charts/safe-minimum-internal-temperatures)
- [ ] **fd66** (ya) Myth: Freezing makes recalled food safe. Fix: Throw away recalled foods and clean the fridge where you kept them. Source: [CDC: Preventing Food Poisoning](https://www.cdc.gov/food-safety/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **fd67** (ya) Myth: Resting is just for looks. Fix: Rest time lets the innermost parts become fully and safely cooked. Source: [FoodSafety.gov: Safe Minimum Internal Temperatures](https://www.foodsafety.gov/food-safety-charts/safe-minimum-internal-temperatures)
- [ ] **fd68** (ya) Myth: Gloves cover it. Fix: Food workers must wash hands after using the toilet. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd69** (ya) Myth: Pets are clean enough. Fix: Wash hands after handling animals, including service animals. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)
- [ ] **fd70** (ya) Myth: Refreezing is always fine. Fix: Packaged frozen food that a kitchen thaws may not be refrozen. Source: [TB MED 530 / NAVMED P-5010-1: Tri-Service Food Code](https://armypubs.army.mil/epubs/DR_pubs/DR_a/pdf/web/ARN15052_TB_MED_530_FINAL.pdf)

## Country Living: Animals, Water and Home Health (64 cards)

Reviewed by: ________  Date: ________

- [ ] **hm01** (both) Myth: Clean looking birds carry no germs. Fix: Wash hands with soap and running water after touching poultry or their area. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm02** (both) Myth: Nothing works without a sink. Fix: Use hand sanitizer if soap and water are not handy. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm03** (both) Myth: Healthy looking birds are germ free. Fix: Poultry can look healthy and clean and still spread germs. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm04** (both) Myth: Pet chickens are safe to cuddle. Fix: CDC says don't kiss or snuggle poultry and then touch your face. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm05** (both) Myth: A tame hen is fine indoors. Fix: Keep poultry out of the house, mostly where food is made. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm06** (both) Myth: Any shoes are fine. Fix: Set aside shoes for poultry care and keep them outside the house. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm07** (both) Myth: Eggs can sit for days. Fix: Eggs left in the nest can get dirty or break. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm08** (both) Myth: Cooking makes cracked eggs fine. Fix: Throw away cracked eggs; germs get in through the crack. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm09** (both) Myth: Cold water washes germs off. Fix: Clean dirty eggs with sandpaper, a brush or cloth; don't wash warm fresh eggs. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm10** (both) Myth: Farm eggs never need the fridge. Fix: Refrigerate eggs after collecting them. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm11** (both) Myth: Runny yolks are safe on the farm. Fix: Cook eggs until both yolk and white are firm. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm12** (both) Myth: Warm is enough for eggs. Fix: Egg dishes should be cooked to 160°F or hotter. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm13** (both) Myth: Chicks are a great toddler pet. Fix: Children under 5 should not handle chicks or other poultry. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm14** (both) Myth: Chicks make great gifts. Fix: CDC says don't give chicks or ducklings to young children as gifts. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm15** (both) Myth: Small pecks don't matter. Fix: Wash poultry bites or scratches with soap and warm water right away. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm16** (both) Myth: New birds can mix right away. Fix: Keep new poultry apart at least 30 days first. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm17** (both) Myth: Disinfectant works on anything. Fix: Most disinfectants don't work on dirty surfaces; clean first. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm18** (both) Myth: The sink is easiest. Fix: Clean poultry equipment outside. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm19** (both) Myth: Bare hands are fine for droppings. Fix: Wear gloves when cleaning poultry houses. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm20** (both) Myth: Roosters are banned for germs. Fix: Many cities ban roosters because crowing breaks noise rules. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm21** (both) Myth: Goats stay where you put them. Fix: Fencing is the most important part of goat facilities. Source: [UW Extension 4-H: Guide to Raising Healthy Goats (A3858-05)](https://4h.extension.wisc.edu/files/2026/02/Guide-to-Raising-Healthy-Goats.pdf)
- [ ] **hm22** (both) Myth: Goats need a warm barn. Fix: A simple three sided shed facing away from winter wind works if it isn't drafty. Source: [UW Extension 4-H: Guide to Raising Healthy Goats (A3858-05)](https://4h.extension.wisc.edu/files/2026/02/Guide-to-Raising-Healthy-Goats.pdf)
- [ ] **hm23** (both) Myth: Summer needs no care. Fix: In summer, shade and fly control matter most. Source: [UW Extension 4-H: Guide to Raising Healthy Goats (A3858-05)](https://4h.extension.wisc.edu/files/2026/02/Guide-to-Raising-Healthy-Goats.pdf)
- [ ] **hm24** (both) Myth: Goats barely drink. Fix: A goat can drink between half a gallon and 4 gallons a day. Source: [UW Extension 4-H: Guide to Raising Healthy Goats (A3858-05)](https://4h.extension.wisc.edu/files/2026/02/Guide-to-Raising-Healthy-Goats.pdf)
- [ ] **hm25** (both) Myth: Any tank works. Fix: Low sides let young kids drink without choking. Source: [UW Extension 4-H: Guide to Raising Healthy Goats (A3858-05)](https://4h.extension.wisc.edu/files/2026/02/Guide-to-Raising-Healthy-Goats.pdf)
- [ ] **hm26** (both) Myth: Richer pasture is always better. Fix: Lush legume pasture like clover or alfalfa can cause bloat. Source: [UW Extension 4-H: Guide to Raising Healthy Goats (A3858-05)](https://4h.extension.wisc.edu/files/2026/02/Guide-to-Raising-Healthy-Goats.pdf)
- [ ] **hm27** (both) Myth: A quiet loner goat is fine. Fix: A healthy goat chews its cud, eats well and has bright eyes. Source: [UC ANR (NC State Extension text): Basic Meat Goat Facts](https://www.ucanr.edu/sites/default/files/2020-12/341671.pdf)
- [ ] **hm28** (both) Myth: Not eating for a day is normal. Fix: Going off feed and water is a sign of illness. Source: [UC ANR (NC State Extension text): Basic Meat Goat Facts](https://www.ucanr.edu/sites/default/files/2020-12/341671.pdf)
- [ ] **hm29** (both) Myth: Goats like time alone. Fix: Standing apart from the group is a sign of illness. Source: [UC ANR (NC State Extension text): Basic Meat Goat Facts](https://www.ucanr.edu/sites/default/files/2020-12/341671.pdf)
- [ ] **hm30** (both) Myth: Pale gums are normal. Fix: Pale mucosa of eyes and mouth is a sign of illness. Source: [UC ANR (NC State Extension text): Basic Meat Goat Facts](https://www.ucanr.edu/sites/default/files/2020-12/341671.pdf)
- [ ] **hm31** (both) Myth: Goats take care of themselves. Fix: Observe your animals daily. Source: [UC ANR (NC State Extension text): Basic Meat Goat Facts](https://www.ucanr.edu/sites/default/files/2020-12/341671.pdf)
- [ ] **hm32** (both) Myth: New goats can mix at once. Fix: Isolate new animals for a month. Source: [UC ANR (NC State Extension text): Basic Meat Goat Facts](https://www.ucanr.edu/sites/default/files/2020-12/341671.pdf)
- [ ] **hm33** (both) Myth: Goats can't be trained. Fix: Goats are social and curious and can be trained to follow a lead or pail of grain. Source: [UW Extension 4-H: Guide to Raising Healthy Goats (A3858-05)](https://4h.extension.wisc.edu/files/2026/02/Guide-to-Raising-Healthy-Goats.pdf)
- [ ] **hm34** (both) Myth: Radiator water is clean. Fix: Never use radiator or boiler water from home heating for drinking. Source: [CDC: Making Water Safe in an Emergency](https://www.cdc.gov/water-emergency/about/)
- [ ] **hm35** (both) Myth: Thirst comes last. Fix: Thirst is often a first, early sign of dehydration. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 13 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_13.pdf)
- [ ] **hm36** (both) Myth: Dark urine is normal. Fix: Little or no urine, or dark yellow urine, is a sign of dehydration. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 13 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_13.pdf)
- [ ] **hm37** (both) Myth: Slow skin is fine. Fix: Skin that doesn't fall right back is a sign of dehydration. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 13 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_13.pdf)
- [ ] **hm38** (both) Myth: Adults dry out first. Fix: Dehydration develops fastest and is most dangerous in very young children. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 13 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_13.pdf)
- [ ] **hm39** (both) Myth: Wait for signs of dehydration. Fix: With watery diarrhea, don't wait for signs; give fluids quickly. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 13 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_13.pdf)
- [ ] **hm40** (both) Myth: Sweating it out helps. Fix: Do not cover a child with fever in too many clothes or blankets. Source: [NHS: Fever in Children](https://www.nhs.uk/conditions/fever-in-children/)
- [ ] **hm41** (both) Myth: Cool sponging is the best way to bring a fever down. Fix: Do not undress a child or sponge them down to cool them. Give plenty of fluids. Source: [NHS: Fever in Children](https://www.nhs.uk/conditions/fever-in-children/)
- [ ] **hm42** (both) Myth: Fever seizures always pass on their own. Fix: Call 911 if a child with fever has a first seizure, is hard to wake, or has trouble breathing. Source: [NHS: Fever in Children](https://www.nhs.uk/conditions/fever-in-children/)
- [ ] **hm43** (both) Myth: Alcohol first. Fix: First, wash your hands well with soap and water. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 10 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_10.pdf)
- [ ] **hm44** (both) Myth: Alcohol is best for wounds. Fix: Never put alcohol or iodine tincture right into a wound; it damages flesh. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 10 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_10.pdf)
- [ ] **hm45** (both) Myth: Pus means healing. Fix: A wound with pus, redness, swelling, heat or bad smell is infected. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 10 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_10.pdf)
- [ ] **hm46** (ya) Myth: Red lines are normal. Fix: A red line above a wound means infection is spreading. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 10 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_10.pdf)
- [ ] **hm47** (ya) Myth: Tip the head back. Fix: Sit up and pinch the nose; do not tip the head back. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 10 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_10.pdf)
- [ ] **hm48** (ya) Myth: A few seconds is enough. Fix: Pinch firmly for 10 minutes or until it stops. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 10 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_10.pdf)
- [ ] **hm49** (ya) Myth: Any bottle is fine. Fix: Never keep fuel or poisons in soft drink bottles. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 10 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_10.pdf)
- [ ] **hm50** (ya) Myth: Any seller is the same. Fix: CDC says buy poultry from hatcheries in the USDA NPIP plan. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm51** (ya) Myth: Droppings are harmless. Fix: Don't pick up droppings bare handed, and wash hands after. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm52** (ya) Myth: Hooves never need care. Fix: Hooves may need trimming two or more times a year; bad trims can lame a goat. Source: [UW Extension 4-H: Guide to Raising Healthy Goats (A3858-05)](https://4h.extension.wisc.edu/files/2026/02/Guide-to-Raising-Healthy-Goats.pdf)
- [ ] **hm53** (ya) Myth: The biggest male leads. Fix: Goats move in family groups, older females first. Source: [UW Extension 4-H: Guide to Raising Healthy Goats (A3858-05)](https://4h.extension.wisc.edu/files/2026/02/Guide-to-Raising-Healthy-Goats.pdf)
- [ ] **hm54** (ya) Myth: There's no other way. Fix: CDC says you can also let cloudy water settle before treating it. Source: [CDC: Making Water Safe in an Emergency](https://www.cdc.gov/water-emergency/about/)
- [ ] **hm55** (ya) Myth: Any container is fine. Fix: Store boiled water in clean, sanitized containers with tight covers. Source: [CDC: Making Water Safe in an Emergency](https://www.cdc.gov/water-emergency/about/)
- [ ] **hm56** (adult) Myth: Goats run like people. Fix: Normal goat temperature is about 101.7°F to 104.5°F. Source: [UC ANR (NC State Extension text): Basic Meat Goat Facts](https://www.ucanr.edu/sites/default/files/2020-12/341671.pdf)
- [ ] **hm57** (adult) Myth: A shot prevents CAE. Fix: There is no vaccine or cure for CAE. Source: [UW Extension 4-H: Guide to Raising Healthy Goats (A3858-05)](https://4h.extension.wisc.edu/files/2026/02/Guide-to-Raising-Healthy-Goats.pdf)
- [ ] **hm58** (adult) Myth: Deworm everyone always. Fix: Have a fecal sample tested to check for parasitic worms. Source: [UW Extension 4-H: Guide to Raising Healthy Goats (A3858-05)](https://4h.extension.wisc.edu/files/2026/02/Guide-to-Raising-Healthy-Goats.pdf)
- [ ] **hm59** (adult) Myth: Any age works. Fix: Dehorning should be done within 5 days after birth. Source: [UW Extension 4-H: Guide to Raising Healthy Goats (A3858-05)](https://4h.extension.wisc.edu/files/2026/02/Guide-to-Raising-Healthy-Goats.pdf)
- [ ] **hm60** (adult) Myth: Restock right away. Fix: Consider waiting at least 30 days before replacing poultry. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm61** (adult) Myth: Bird scratches can't cause tetanus. Fix: See a doctor if it's been over 5 years since your last tetanus shot. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm62** (adult) Myth: Sharing is fine. Fix: CDC advises avoiding sharing tools with neighbors. Source: [CDC: Backyard Poultry](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-pets/about/backyard-poultry.html)
- [ ] **hm64** (adult) Myth: Mud is an old cure. Fix: Never put mud or feces on a wound; it can cause tetanus. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 10 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_10.pdf)
- [ ] **hm65** (adult) Myth: Leave it on until healed. Fix: Change the gauze or cloth every day and check for infection. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 10 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_10.pdf)

## Kitchen and Food Myths (78 cards)

Reviewed by: ________  Date: ________

- [ ] **kf01** (both) Myth: Rinsing raw chicken makes it cleaner. Fix: Raw chicken is ready to cook. Do not wash it. Source: [CDC: Chicken and Food Poisoning](https://www.cdc.gov/food-safety/foods/chicken.html)
- [ ] **kf02** (both) Myth: Water puts out a grease fire. Fix: Slide a lid over the pan and turn off the burner. Never use water. Source: [NFPA: Cooking safety](https://www.nfpa.org/education-and-research/home-fire-safety/cooking)
- [ ] **kf03** (both) Myth: Lift the lid soon to check the fire. Fix: Do not remove the lid. Let the pan cool for a long time. Source: [NFPA: Cooking safety](https://www.nfpa.org/education-and-research/home-fire-safety/cooking)
- [ ] **kf04** (both) Myth: Thawing on the counter is fine. Fix: Use the fridge, cold water, or the microwave. Never the counter. Source: [USDA FSIS: The Big Thaw](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/big-thaw-safe-defrosting-methods)
- [ ] **kf05** (both) Myth: Brown inside means a burger is done. Fix: Use a food thermometer. Ground beef needs 160 F. Source: [USDA FSIS: Kitchen Thermometers](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/kitchen-thermometers)
- [ ] **kf06** (both) Myth: Pink burger is always undercooked. Fix: At 160 F it is safe, even if pink. Source: [USDA FSIS: The Color of Meat and Poultry](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/color-meat-and-poultry)
- [ ] **kf07** (both) Myth: Pink poultry is never safe. Fix: All the meat is safe once every part reaches 165 F. Source: [USDA FSIS: The Color of Meat and Poultry](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/color-meat-and-poultry)
- [ ] **kf08** (both) Myth: Cool hot food on the counter first. Fix: Hot food can go right in the fridge, or an ice bath first. Source: [USDA FSIS: Leftovers and Food Safety](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/leftovers-and-food-safety)
- [ ] **kf09** (both) Myth: Just put the whole pot in the fridge. Fix: Divide it into small, shallow containers so it cools fast. Source: [USDA FSIS: Leftovers and Food Safety](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/leftovers-and-food-safety)
- [ ] **kf10** (both) Myth: Leftovers last a week. Fix: Eat them within 3 to 4 days, or freeze them. Source: [USDA FSIS: Leftovers and Food Safety](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/leftovers-and-food-safety)
- [ ] **kf11** (both) Myth: Food is fine out if it looks OK. Fix: Never more than 2 hours, or 1 hour above 90 F. Source: [USDA FSIS: Danger Zone 40 F to 140 F](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/danger-zone-40f-140f)
- [ ] **kf12** (both) Myth: Cooked food left out overnight is fine. Fix: Throw it out. Source: [USDA FSIS: How Temperatures Affect Food](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/how-temperatures-affect-food)
- [ ] **kf13** (both) Myth: Cut the moldy spot off bread. Fix: Throw out moldy bread. Source: [USDA FSIS: Molds on Food](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/molds-food-are-they-dangerous)
- [ ] **kf14** (both) Myth: Any mold means toss hard cheese. Fix: For hard cheese, cut off at least 1 inch around and below the spot. Source: [USDA FSIS: Molds on Food](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/molds-food-are-they-dangerous)
- [ ] **kf15** (both) Myth: Sniff moldy food to check it. Fix: Do not sniff moldy food. Source: [USDA FSIS: Molds on Food](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/molds-food-are-they-dangerous)
- [ ] **kf16** (both) Myth: Past the date means unsafe. Fix: "Best if Used By" is about best flavor, not safety. Check for spoilage. Source: [USDA FSIS: Food Product Dating](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/food-product-dating)
- [ ] **kf17** (both) Myth: Sell-by means eat by. Fix: Sell-By is for the store. It is not a safety date. Source: [USDA FSIS: Food Product Dating](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/food-product-dating)
- [ ] **kf18** (both) Myth: A color change means meat is bad. Fix: Color change alone does not mean spoiled. Look for off smell, sticky or slimy feel. Source: [USDA FSIS: The Color of Meat and Poultry](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/color-meat-and-poultry)
- [ ] **kf19** (both) Myth: Freezer burn makes food unsafe. Fix: It is safe. Those spots are just dry and tasteless. Source: [USDA FSIS: The Color of Meat and Poultry](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/color-meat-and-poultry)
- [ ] **kf20** (both) Myth: Frozen food goes bad after a while. Fix: Food kept frozen stays safe. Time only hurts quality. Source: [USDA FSIS: Freezing and Food Safety](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/freezing-and-food-safety)
- [ ] **kf21** (both) Myth: Pork must be cooked to well done. Fix: Pork chops are safe at 145 F with a 3 minute rest. Source: [FoodSafety.gov: Safe Minimum Internal Temperatures](https://www.foodsafety.gov/food-safety-charts/safe-minimum-internal-temperatures)
- [ ] **kf22** (both) Myth: Eggs belong in the fridge door. Fix: Keep them in the carton on a main shelf, not the door. Source: [FoodSafety.gov: 4 Steps to Food Safety](https://www.foodsafety.gov/keep-food-safe/4-steps-to-food-safety)
- [ ] **kf23** (both) Myth: You need antibacterial soap. Fix: Use plain soap and water. Scrub for 20 seconds. Source: [FoodSafety.gov: 4 Steps to Food Safety](https://www.foodsafety.gov/keep-food-safe/4-steps-to-food-safety)
- [ ] **kf24** (both) Myth: A quick rinse is enough. Fix: Scrub at least 20 seconds. Hum "Happy Birthday" twice. Source: [CDC: About Handwashing](https://www.cdc.gov/clean-hands/about/index.html)
- [ ] **kf25** (both) Myth: Wiping a board is enough. Fix: Use one board for raw meat and another for food you will not cook. Source: [FoodSafety.gov: 4 Steps to Food Safety](https://www.foodsafety.gov/keep-food-safe/4-steps-to-food-safety)
- [ ] **kf26** (both) Myth: Always rewash bagged salad. Fix: Produce labeled pre-washed does not need washing again. Source: [FoodSafety.gov: 4 Steps to Food Safety](https://www.foodsafety.gov/keep-food-safe/4-steps-to-food-safety)
- [ ] **kf27** (both) Myth: Wash fruit with soap. Fix: Wash under running water. Soap and produce washes are not advised. Source: [FDA: Selecting and Serving Produce Safely](https://www.fda.gov/food/buy-store-serve-safe-food/selecting-and-serving-produce-safely)
- [ ] **kf28** (both) Myth: No need to wash produce you peel. Fix: Wash it anyway. Source: [FDA: Selecting and Serving Produce Safely](https://www.fda.gov/food/buy-store-serve-safe-food/selecting-and-serving-produce-safely)
- [ ] **kf29** (both) Myth: If it smells fine, it is safe. Fix: Throw out swollen, rusted, or badly dented cans. Source: [USDA FSIS: Food Product Dating](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/food-product-dating)
- [ ] **kf30** (both) Myth: Touching the bone gives the best reading. Fix: Put it in the thickest part, away from bone, fat, or gristle. Source: [FoodSafety.gov: 4 Steps to Food Safety](https://www.foodsafety.gov/keep-food-safe/4-steps-to-food-safety)
- [ ] **kf31** (both) Myth: Microwave food is done when it beeps. Fix: Let it sit a few minutes so cold spots heat up. Source: [CDC: Four Steps to Food Safety](https://www.cdc.gov/food-safety/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **kf32** (both) Myth: Smoke alarms last forever. Fix: Get new smoke alarms every 10 years. Source: [US Fire Administration: Smoke alarms](https://www.usfa.fema.gov/prevention/home-fires/prepare-for-fire/smoke-alarms/)
- [ ] **kf33** (both) Myth: You have plenty of time to get out. Fix: You may have less than 2 minutes to get everyone out. Source: [US Fire Administration: Smoke alarms](https://www.usfa.fema.gov/prevention/home-fires/prepare-for-fire/smoke-alarms/)
- [ ] **kf34** (both) Myth: Aim at the flames. Fix: Remember PASS: Pull, Aim low at the base, Squeeze, Sweep. Source: [US Fire Administration: Fire extinguishers](https://www.usfa.fema.gov/prevention/home-fires/prepare-for-fire/fire-extinguishers/)
- [ ] **kf35** (both) Myth: You must thaw meat before cooking. Fix: It is safe to cook from frozen. Plan about 50% more time. Source: [USDA FSIS: The Big Thaw](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/big-thaw-safe-defrosting-methods)
- [ ] **kf50** (ya) Myth: Hot water is a fine way to thaw meat. Fix: Use cold water in a sealed bag. Change it every 30 minutes. Source: [USDA FSIS: The Big Thaw](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/big-thaw-safe-defrosting-methods)
- [ ] **kf51** (ya) Myth: Warm leftovers are safe enough. Fix: Reheat leftovers to 165 F. Source: [USDA FSIS: Leftovers and Food Safety](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/leftovers-and-food-safety)
- [ ] **kf52** (ya) Myth: The 2 hour rule always applies. Fix: Above 90 F, only 1 hour. Source: [USDA FSIS: Danger Zone 40 F to 140 F](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/danger-zone-40f-140f)
- [ ] **kf53** (ya) Myth: Scoop the mold off jam. Fix: Throw out moldy jam and jelly. Source: [USDA FSIS: Molds on Food](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/molds-food-are-they-dangerous)
- [ ] **kf54** (ya) Myth: Pick mold out of shredded cheese. Fix: Throw out moldy shredded, sliced, or crumbled cheese. Source: [USDA FSIS: Molds on Food](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/molds-food-are-they-dangerous)
- [ ] **kf55** (ya) Myth: Raw ground beef keeps a week. Fix: Cook or freeze raw ground meat within 1 to 2 days. Source: [FoodSafety.gov: Cold Food Storage Chart](https://www.foodsafety.gov/food-safety-charts/cold-food-storage-charts)
- [ ] **kf56** (ya) Myth: Raw chicken keeps most of a week. Fix: Raw chicken pieces: 1 to 2 days in the fridge. Source: [FoodSafety.gov: Cold Food Storage Chart](https://www.foodsafety.gov/food-safety-charts/cold-food-storage-charts)
- [ ] **kf57** (ya) Myth: Hot dogs keep for a month. Fix: Opened hot dogs: about 1 week. Source: [FoodSafety.gov: Cold Food Storage Chart](https://www.foodsafety.gov/food-safety-charts/cold-food-storage-charts)
- [ ] **kf58** (ya) Myth: Mayo salads keep for weeks. Fix: Tuna, egg, or chicken salad: 3 to 4 days. Source: [FoodSafety.gov: Cold Food Storage Chart](https://www.foodsafety.gov/food-safety-charts/cold-food-storage-charts)
- [ ] **kf59** (ya) Myth: Garlic in oil keeps on the shelf. Fix: Refrigerate homemade garlic or herb oil. Throw it out after 4 days. Source: [CDC: Preventing Botulism](https://www.cdc.gov/botulism/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **kf60** (ya) Myth: Cooking tired or after drinks is fine. Fix: If you are sleepy or have had alcohol, do not use the stove. Source: [NFPA: Cooking safety](https://www.nfpa.org/education-and-research/home-fire-safety/cooking)
- [ ] **kf61** (ya) Myth: One ice pack keeps lunch cold. Fix: Use at least two cold sources, like two gel packs or a pack plus a frozen water bottle. Source: [USDA FSIS: Keeping Bag Lunches Safe](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/keeping-bag-lunches-safe)
- [ ] **kf62** (ya) Myth: Reusing paper lunch bags is fine. Fix: Throw out used food packaging and paper bags. Source: [USDA FSIS: Keeping Bag Lunches Safe](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/keeping-bag-lunches-safe)
- [ ] **kf63** (ya) Myth: Every packed lunch needs ice. Fix: Peanut butter, jelly, bread, whole fruit, and hard cheese are fine without ice. Source: [USDA FSIS: Keeping Bag Lunches Safe](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/keeping-bag-lunches-safe)
- [ ] **kf64** (ya) Myth: Fish is done when the outside browns. Fix: Fish is done at 145 F, or when it is not see-through and flakes with a fork. Source: [CDC: Four Steps to Food Safety](https://www.cdc.gov/food-safety/prevention/index.html)
- [ ] **kf65** (ya) Myth: Checking one piece is enough. Fix: Check each piece. They cook at different speeds. Source: [USDA FSIS: Kitchen Thermometers](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/kitchen-thermometers)
- [ ] **kf66** (ya) Myth: Grill marks show a burger is cooked. Fix: Grill marks are not a sign of safety. Use a thermometer. Source: [USDA FSIS: Kitchen Thermometers](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/kitchen-thermometers)
- [ ] **kf67** (ya) Myth: Wiping hands is good enough. Fix: If no soap and water, use sanitizer with at least 60% alcohol. Source: [CDC: About Handwashing](https://www.cdc.gov/clean-hands/about/index.html)
- [ ] **kf68** (ya) Myth: You cannot freeze leftovers. Fix: Freeze leftovers. Best quality for 3 to 4 months. Source: [USDA FSIS: Leftovers and Food Safety](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/leftovers-and-food-safety)
- [ ] **kf69** (ya) Myth: Runny eggs are always safe. Fix: FDA says cook eggs until both yolk and white are firm. Source: [FDA: What You Need to Know About Egg Safety](https://www.fda.gov/food/buy-store-serve-safe-food/what-you-need-know-about-egg-safety)
- [ ] **kf70** (ya) Myth: Frozen leftovers must thaw first. Fix: It is safe to reheat frozen leftovers without thawing. Source: [USDA FSIS: Leftovers and Food Safety](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/leftovers-and-food-safety)
- [ ] **kf80** (adult) Myth: A turkey thaws overnight in the fridge. Fix: Allow at least 1 day for every 5 pounds. Source: [USDA FSIS: The Big Thaw](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/big-thaw-safe-defrosting-methods)
- [ ] **kf81** (adult) Myth: Frozen meat in a slow cooker is fine. Fix: Always thaw meat or poultry before it goes in a slow cooker. Source: [USDA FSIS: Slow Cookers and Food Safety](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/slow-cookers-and-food-safety)
- [ ] **kf82** (adult) Myth: Slow cookers are great for reheating. Fix: Reheating leftovers in a slow cooker is not advised. Use the stove, oven, or microwave. Source: [USDA FSIS: Slow Cookers and Food Safety](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/slow-cookers-and-food-safety)
- [ ] **kf83** (adult) Myth: If it looks done, it is safe. Fix: If you were away when the power went out, throw it away. Source: [USDA FSIS: Slow Cookers and Food Safety](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/slow-cookers-and-food-safety)
- [ ] **kf84** (adult) Myth: Hunted Easter eggs are fine to eat. Fix: Do not eat eggs after hiding or playing with them. Source: [FDA: What You Need to Know About Egg Safety](https://www.fda.gov/food/buy-store-serve-safe-food/what-you-need-know-about-egg-safety)
- [ ] **kf85** (adult) Myth: Fresh eggs are safe raw. Fix: For raw egg recipes, use pasteurized eggs. Source: [FDA: What You Need to Know About Egg Safety](https://www.fda.gov/food/buy-store-serve-safe-food/what-you-need-know-about-egg-safety)
- [ ] **kf86** (adult) Myth: Eggs only last a week. Fix: Raw eggs in the shell: 3 to 5 weeks in the fridge. Source: [FoodSafety.gov: Cold Food Storage Chart](https://www.foodsafety.gov/food-safety-charts/cold-food-storage-charts)
- [ ] **kf87** (adult) Myth: You can freeze eggs in the shell. Fix: Do not freeze eggs in the shell. Beat yolks and whites together first. Source: [FDA: What You Need to Know About Egg Safety](https://www.fda.gov/food/buy-store-serve-safe-food/what-you-need-know-about-egg-safety)
- [ ] **kf88** (adult) Myth: Boiled eggs keep as long as raw. Fix: Hard-cooked eggs: 1 week in the fridge. Source: [FoodSafety.gov: Cold Food Storage Chart](https://www.foodsafety.gov/food-safety-charts/cold-food-storage-charts)
- [ ] **kf89** (adult) Myth: Kids should use fire extinguishers. Fix: Young children and older adults should not use them. Get out and call 911. Source: [US Fire Administration: Fire extinguishers](https://www.usfa.fema.gov/prevention/home-fires/prepare-for-fire/fire-extinguishers/)
- [ ] **kf90** (adult) Myth: Test smoke alarms once a year. Fix: Test every month. Change 9-volt batteries at least once a year. Source: [US Fire Administration: Smoke alarms](https://www.usfa.fema.gov/prevention/home-fires/prepare-for-fire/smoke-alarms/)
- [ ] **kf91** (adult) Myth: A generator in an open garage is fine. Fix: Use it outside only, at least 20 feet away, exhaust pointed away. Source: [CPSC: Carbon Monoxide Information Center](https://www.cpsc.gov/Safety-Education/Safety-Education-Centers/Carbon-Monoxide-Information-Center)
- [ ] **kf92** (adult) Myth: A few bottles is enough water. Fix: Store 1 gallon per person per day, for several days. Source: [Ready.gov: Build a Kit](https://www.ready.gov/kit)
- [ ] **kf93** (adult) Myth: Ration water in an emergency. Fix: Do not ration unless told to. Drink what you need and find more. Source: [Ready.gov: Water](https://www.ready.gov/water)
- [ ] **kf94** (adult) Myth: Soda or coffee can replace water. Fix: Do not use caffeinated or fizzy drinks instead of water. Source: [Ready.gov: Water](https://www.ready.gov/water)
- [ ] **kf95** (adult) Myth: Any bleach can treat water. Fix: Use only regular unscented bleach. No scented or color-safe kinds. Source: [Ready.gov: Water](https://www.ready.gov/water)
- [ ] **kf96** (adult) Myth: Warm water is safe enough. Fix: Bring to a rolling boil for 1 full minute, then cool. Source: [Ready.gov: Water](https://www.ready.gov/water)
- [ ] **kf97** (adult) Myth: Warm all the way through is enough. Fix: Casseroles should reach 165 F. Source: [FoodSafety.gov: Safe Minimum Internal Temperatures](https://www.foodsafety.gov/food-safety-charts/safe-minimum-internal-temperatures)
- [ ] **kf98** (adult) Myth: Cold dips are fine sitting out. Fix: Keep cold food on ice and swap in small trays often. Source: [USDA FSIS: Leftovers and Food Safety](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/leftovers-and-food-safety)
- [ ] **kf99** (adult) Myth: Packaged lunch kits are fine warm. Fix: Combos with meat, cheese, or cut fruit must stay cold. Source: [USDA FSIS: Keeping Bag Lunches Safe](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/keeping-bag-lunches-safe)
- [ ] **kf100** (adult) Myth: Bacon keeps for a month. Fix: Bacon: about 1 week in the fridge. Source: [FoodSafety.gov: Cold Food Storage Chart](https://www.foodsafety.gov/food-safety-charts/cold-food-storage-charts)
- [ ] **kf101** (adult) Myth: Raw chicken keeps a week. Fix: Raw whole chicken: 1 to 2 days in the fridge. Freeze it if longer. Source: [FoodSafety.gov: Cold Food Storage Chart](https://www.foodsafety.gov/food-safety-charts/cold-food-storage-charts)

## Medicine Safety (70 cards)

Reviewed by: ________  Date: ________

- [ ] **md01** (both) Myth: Green or yellow mucus means you need an antibiotic. Fix: Colds and runny noses are viruses, even with thick green mucus. Antibiotics don't work on viruses. Source: [CDC: Antibiotic Do's and Don'ts](https://www.cdc.gov/antibiotic-use/about/index.html)
- [ ] **md02** (both) Myth: Antibiotics kill any germ. Fix: Antibiotics only treat some infections caused by bacteria. They do not work on viruses. Source: [CDC: Antibiotic Do's and Don'ts](https://www.cdc.gov/antibiotic-use/about/index.html)
- [ ] **md03** (both) Myth: A sore throat always needs antibiotics. Fix: Most sore throats are not helped by antibiotics, except strep throat. Source: [CDC: Antibiotic Do's and Don'ts](https://www.cdc.gov/antibiotic-use/about/index.html)
- [ ] **md04** (both) Myth: A chest cold always needs an antibiotic. Fix: Most chest colds are not helped by antibiotics. Source: [CDC: Antibiotic Do's and Don'ts](https://www.cdc.gov/antibiotic-use/about/index.html)
- [ ] **md05** (both) Myth: Every bacterial infection needs an antibiotic. Fix: Many sinus infections and some ear infections get better without antibiotics. Source: [CDC: Antibiotic Do's and Don'ts](https://www.cdc.gov/antibiotic-use/about/index.html)
- [ ] **md06** (both) Myth: Saving leftover antibiotics is smart. Fix: Don't save antibiotics for later. The wrong medicine can delay the right care and cause side effects. Source: [CDC: Antibiotic Do's and Don'ts](https://www.cdc.gov/antibiotic-use/about/index.html)
- [ ] **md07** (both) Myth: Sharing antibiotics with family is fine. Fix: Don't share antibiotics or take ones made out for someone else. Source: [CDC: Antibiotic Do's and Don'ts](https://www.cdc.gov/antibiotic-use/about/index.html)
- [ ] **md08** (both) Myth: Stop as soon as you feel better. Fix: Take antibiotics exactly as prescribed. Source: [CDC: Antibiotic Do's and Don'ts](https://www.cdc.gov/antibiotic-use/about/index.html)
- [ ] **md09** (both) Myth: Antibiotics are always gentle for kids. Fix: In children, antibiotic side effects are the most common cause of medicine-related ER visits. Source: [CDC: Antibiotic Do's and Don'ts](https://www.cdc.gov/antibiotic-use/about/index.html)
- [ ] **md10** (adult) Myth: Antibiotics only help the gut. Fix: Antibiotics can lead to C. diff, a diarrhea that can badly harm the colon. Source: [CDC: Antibiotic Do's and Don'ts](https://www.cdc.gov/antibiotic-use/about/index.html)
- [ ] **md11** (adult) Myth: Pushing gets you better care. Fix: Never pressure a health worker for an antibiotic. Source: [CDC: Antibiotic Do's and Don'ts](https://www.cdc.gov/antibiotic-use/about/index.html)
- [ ] **md12** (both) Myth: Finish the course no matter what. Fix: A rash, itching or hard breathing means stop it and never take it again. Get medical help. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 7 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_07.pdf)
- [ ] **md13** (both) Myth: Feeling better means you're cured. Fix: Take antibiotics exactly as prescribed. Do not save them for later. Source: [CDC: Antibiotic Do's and Don'ts](https://www.cdc.gov/antibiotic-use/about/index.html)
- [ ] **md14** (both) Myth: More use makes them stronger. Fix: When antibiotics are overused, they stop working as well. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 7 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_07.pdf)
- [ ] **md15** (both) Myth: Just take more. Fix: It may be a different illness, the wrong dose or a resistant germ. Get medical help. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 7 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_07.pdf)
- [ ] **md16** (both) Myth: A shot is stronger and better. Fix: Medicine by mouth often works as well as a shot, and shots carry more risk. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 6 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_06.pdf)
- [ ] **md17** (both) Myth: Store-bought medicine is always safe. Fix: There is some danger in using any medicine. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 6 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_06.pdf)
- [ ] **md18** (both) Myth: The strongest medicine works fastest. Fix: Never use a dangerous medicine for a mild illness. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 6 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_06.pdf)
- [ ] **md19** (both) Myth: Keep going no matter what. Fix: If a medicine doesn't help or causes problems, stop it and ask a health worker. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 6 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_06.pdf)
- [ ] **md20** (adult) Myth: Growth tonics help kids grow. Fix: Growth tonics with steroids can make a child end up shorter. Spend the money on food. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 6 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_06.pdf)
- [ ] **md21** (both) Myth: Everyone needs vitamin pills. Fix: A person who eats a variety of good food usually gets enough vitamins. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 6 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_06.pdf)
- [ ] **md22** (both) Myth: Mixed cough medicines work best. Fix: It is best to avoid cough medicines that both quiet a cough and loosen mucus. Where There Is No Doctor says cough medicines rarely help much. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 6 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_06.pdf)
- [ ] **md23** (both) Myth: Food and juice never change how a medicine works. Fix: Grapefruit juice can cause too much or too little of some medicines in your body. Read the label and ask your doctor or pharmacist. Source: [FDA: Grapefruit Juice and Some Drugs Don't Mix](https://www.fda.gov/consumers/consumer-updates/grapefruit-juice-and-some-drugs-dont-mix)
- [ ] **md24** (both) Myth: A mild reaction means it is safe. Fix: After any allergic reaction, even mild, stop and never take that medicine again. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 6 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_06.pdf)
- [ ] **md25** (adult) Myth: Aspirin is gentle on the stomach. Fix: Aspirin and ibuprofen can make ulcers worse. Acetaminophen does not irritate the stomach. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 6 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_06.pdf)
- [ ] **md26** (adult) Myth: Store medicines are safe in pregnancy. Fix: Always check before taking medicines in pregnancy or breastfeeding. Many can harm the baby. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 6 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_06.pdf)
- [ ] **md27** (adult) Myth: A little bit is fine for a baby. Fix: Be very careful with newborns. Look for medical help before giving any medicine. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 6 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_06.pdf)
- [ ] **md28** (adult) Myth: Liver disease makes medicine work better. Fix: When the liver works poorly, some medicines build up and poison the body. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 6 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_06.pdf)
- [ ] **md29** (adult) Myth: Being dry makes medicine weaker. Fix: Kidneys that are dry or diseased clear medicine less well, so it can build up. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 6 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_06.pdf)
- [ ] **md30** (both) Myth: It is just aspirin by another name. Fix: Outside the US, acetaminophen is commonly called paracetamol. Source: [FDA: Acetaminophen](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/information-drug-class/acetaminophen)
- [ ] **md31** (both) Myth: APAP is a brand of aspirin. Fix: APAP is a short name for acetaminophen on prescription labels. Source: [FDA: Acetaminophen](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/information-drug-class/acetaminophen)
- [ ] **md32** (both) Myth: Two products are fine if each is safe alone. Fix: Don't use more than one acetaminophen product at a time. You could take too much. Source: [FDA: Acetaminophen](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/information-drug-class/acetaminophen)
- [ ] **md33** (both) Myth: A little extra is fine if you are still sick. Fix: Never take more than the label says. Too much can cause liver failure and death. Source: [FDA: Acetaminophen](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/information-drug-class/acetaminophen)
- [ ] **md34** (adult) Myth: Alcohol doesn't matter with pain pills. Fix: Three or more drinks a day while using acetaminophen can cause severe liver damage. Source: [FDA: Acetaminophen](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/information-drug-class/acetaminophen)
- [ ] **md35** (adult) Myth: A smaller amount of the adult kind is fine. Fix: Don't use a product meant for adults if the child is under 12. Source: [FDA: Acetaminophen](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/information-drug-class/acetaminophen)
- [ ] **md36** (adult) Myth: A kitchen spoon is close enough. Fix: Use the dosing device that came with the product, not a kitchen spoon. Source: [FDA: Acetaminophen](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/information-drug-class/acetaminophen)
- [ ] **md37** (both) Myth: If it never caused trouble, it never will. Fix: Serious skin reactions can happen even if you took it before with no problem. Source: [FDA: Acetaminophen](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/information-drug-class/acetaminophen)
- [ ] **md38** (both) Myth: A rash will pass; keep taking it. Fix: Stop the medicine and seek medical attention right away. Source: [FDA: Acetaminophen](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/information-drug-class/acetaminophen)
- [ ] **md39** (both) Myth: You can skip the label if you've used it before. Fix: Always read the label of every medicine before use. Source: [FDA: Acetaminophen](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/information-drug-class/acetaminophen)
- [ ] **md40** (adult) Myth: Stop all medicine as soon as you are pregnant. Fix: Do not stop prescribed drugs without first talking to a health care worker. Source: [FDA: Acetaminophen](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/information-drug-class/acetaminophen)
- [ ] **md41** (both) Myth: Expired medicine works just the same. Fix: Once it expires, there is no promise it is safe or works. Don't use it. Source: [FDA: Don't Be Tempted to Use Expired Medicines](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/special-features/dont-be-tempted-use-expired-medicines)
- [ ] **md42** (both) Myth: Old antibiotics only get stronger. Fix: Weak, expired antibiotics can fail to treat infections and add to resistance. Source: [FDA: Don't Be Tempted to Use Expired Medicines](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/special-features/dont-be-tempted-use-expired-medicines)
- [ ] **md43** (both) Myth: The bathroom cabinet is ideal. Fix: A damp bathroom cabinet can make medicine work less well. Source: [FDA: Don't Be Tempted to Use Expired Medicines](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/special-features/dont-be-tempted-use-expired-medicines)
- [ ] **md44** (adult) Myth: Any kitchen spot is fine. Fix: Keep it away from hot appliances and the sink, and out of reach of children. Source: [FDA: Don't Be Tempted to Use Expired Medicines](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/special-features/dont-be-tempted-use-expired-medicines)
- [ ] **md45** (both) Myth: You must call the pharmacy. Fix: It is printed on the label or stamped on the bottle or box, sometimes after "EXP." Source: [FDA: Don't Be Tempted to Use Expired Medicines](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/special-features/dont-be-tempted-use-expired-medicines)
- [ ] **md46** (both) Myth: Flush all old pills down the toilet. Fix: The best option is a drug take-back site or a prepaid mail-back envelope. Source: [FDA: Disposal of Unused Medicines](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/safe-disposal-medicines/disposal-unused-medicines-what-you-should-know)
- [ ] **md47** (both) Myth: There's nowhere to take them. Fix: Take-back sites are often at a local pharmacy or police station. Source: [FDA: Disposal of Unused Medicines](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/safe-disposal-medicines/disposal-unused-medicines-what-you-should-know)
- [ ] **md48** (both) Myth: Flushing is always okay. Fix: Don't flush medicine unless it is on the FDA Flush List. Source: [FDA: Disposal of Unused Medicines](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/safe-disposal-medicines/disposal-unused-medicines-what-you-should-know)
- [ ] **md49** (both) Myth: Crush the pills first. Fix: Mix them, uncrushed, with dirt, cat litter or coffee grounds, and seal in a bag. Source: [FDA: Disposal of Unused Medicines](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/safe-disposal-medicines/disposal-unused-medicines-what-you-should-know)
- [ ] **md50** (adult) Myth: The label doesn't matter. Fix: Scratch out personal information on the empty package. Source: [FDA: Disposal of Unused Medicines](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/safe-disposal-medicines/disposal-unused-medicines-what-you-should-know)
- [ ] **md51** (both) Myth: The list is about cost. Fix: Flush List drugs can be very dangerous with just one dose if a child or pet gets them. Source: [FDA: Disposal of Unused Medicines](https://www.fda.gov/drugs/safe-disposal-medicines/disposal-unused-medicines-what-you-should-know)
- [ ] **md52** (both) Myth: It is a mild rash. Fix: Anaphylaxis is a life-threatening allergic reaction. Source: [MedlinePlus: Anaphylaxis](https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000844.htm)
- [ ] **md53** (both) Myth: It builds slowly over days. Fix: Symptoms often start within seconds or minutes. Source: [MedlinePlus: Anaphylaxis](https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000844.htm)
- [ ] **md54** (both) Myth: Wait to see if it passes. Fix: Anaphylaxis is an emergency. Call 911 right away. Source: [MedlinePlus: Anaphylaxis](https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000844.htm)
- [ ] **md55** (both) Myth: Voice changes don't matter. Fix: A very hoarse or whispered voice can mean dangerous throat swelling. Source: [MedlinePlus: Anaphylaxis](https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000844.htm)
- [ ] **md56** (both) Myth: Tweezers are best. Fix: Scrape it off with something firm like a fingernail or card. Squeezing pushes in more venom. Source: [MedlinePlus: Anaphylaxis](https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000844.htm)
- [ ] **md57** (both) Myth: Allergy pills are the first fix. Fix: Don't give medicine by mouth to someone struggling to breathe. Use their epinephrine if they have it. Source: [MedlinePlus: Anaphylaxis](https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000844.htm)
- [ ] **md58** (both) Myth: Wait for the ambulance before using it. Fix: Their epinephrine should be used right away if it is available. Call 911. Source: [MedlinePlus: Anaphylaxis](https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000844.htm)
- [ ] **md59** (adult) Myth: Allergy shots give full protection. Fix: Don't assume past allergy shots give full protection. Source: [MedlinePlus: Anaphylaxis](https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000844.htm)
- [ ] **md60** (both) Myth: A pillow helps them breathe. Fix: Don't put a pillow under the head if they have trouble breathing. It can block the airway. Source: [MedlinePlus: Anaphylaxis](https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000844.htm)
- [ ] **md61** (ya) Myth: Pollen is the top cause. Fix: Pollen and other breathed-in allergens rarely cause anaphylaxis. Source: [MedlinePlus: Anaphylaxis](https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000844.htm)
- [ ] **md62** (ya) Myth: Nothing; you'll remember to tell people. Fix: People who have had serious reactions should wear a medical ID tag or bracelet. Source: [MedlinePlus: Anaphylaxis](https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000844.htm)
- [ ] **md63** (ya) Myth: Antibiotics help with the flu. Fix: Antibiotics will not make you feel better if you have a virus. Source: [CDC: Antibiotic Do's and Don'ts](https://www.cdc.gov/antibiotic-use/about/index.html)
- [ ] **md64** (ya) Myth: Leftover antibiotics are fine to share. Fix: Don't take antibiotics prescribed for someone else. Source: [CDC: Antibiotic Do's and Don'ts](https://www.cdc.gov/antibiotic-use/about/index.html)
- [ ] **md65** (ya) Myth: A quick splash works. Fix: Wash with soap and water for at least 20 seconds, or use sanitizer with at least 60% alcohol. Source: [CDC: Antibiotic Do's and Don'ts](https://www.cdc.gov/antibiotic-use/about/index.html)
- [ ] **md66** (ya) Myth: Steroids are a safe way to bulk up. Fix: Anabolic steroids cause very dangerous side effects. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 6 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_06.pdf)
- [ ] **md67** (ya) Myth: An IV drip gives strength. Fix: IV fluids are just water with salt or sugar, with less energy than a candy bar. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 6 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_06.pdf)
- [ ] **md68** (ya) Myth: Always ask for the strongest medicine. Fix: Tell your health worker you only want medicine if it is truly needed. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 6 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_06.pdf)
- [ ] **md69** (ya) Myth: Generic medicine is weaker. Fix: The generic is the same medicine as the brand name and often costs less. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 7 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_07.pdf)
- [ ] **md70** (ya) Myth: They work in hours. Fix: For most common infections, antibiotics bring some improvement in a day or two. Source: [Hesperian, Where There Is No Doctor (2025), ch. 7 (reworded)](https://hesperian.org/wp-content/uploads/pdf/en_wtnd_2025/en_wtnd_2025_07.pdf)

## Money Myths (73 cards)

Reviewed by: ________  Date: ________

- [ ] **mn01** (both) Myth: Paying the minimum keeps you fine. Fix: Only paying the minimum can take years to pay off. Pay more when you can. Source: [CFPB: Minimum payment box on my credit card bill](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/a-box-on-my-credit-card-bill-says-that-i-will-pay-off-the-balance-in-three-years-if-i-pay-a-certain-amount-what-does-that-mean-do-i-have-to-pay-that-much-if-i-pay-that-much-and-make-new-purchases-will-i-still-owe-nothing-after-three-years-en-36/)
- [ ] **mn02** (both) Myth: Checking your own credit hurts your score. Fix: Checking your own report does not hurt your score. Source: [CFPB: Does requesting my credit report hurt my score?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/does-requesting-my-credit-report-hurt-my-credit-score-en-1229/)
- [ ] **mn03** (both) Myth: Credit reports cost money. Fix: You can see each of the three reports free once a week at AnnualCreditReport.com. Source: [CFPB: How do I get a free copy of my credit reports?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/how-do-i-get-a-free-copy-of-my-credit-reports-en-5/)
- [ ] **mn04** (both) Myth: Any site saying free is free. Fix: Some sites charge you later. Use AnnualCreditReport.com. Source: [CFPB: How do I get a free copy of my credit reports?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/how-do-i-get-a-free-copy-of-my-credit-reports-en-5/)
- [ ] **mn05** (both) Myth: Every lender check hurts your score. Fix: Checks for the same loan type within 14 to 45 days usually count as one. Source: [CFPB: What kind of credit inquiry has no effect on my score?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-kind-of-credit-inquiry-has-no-effect-on-my-credit-score-en-321/)
- [ ] **mn06** (both) Myth: A higher bracket taxes all your income more. Fix: You pay the higher rate only on the part in the new bracket. Source: [IRS: Federal income tax rates and brackets](https://www.irs.gov/filing/federal-income-tax-rates-and-brackets)
- [ ] **mn07** (both) Myth: Debit and credit cards protect you the same. Fix: Credit card loss is capped at $50. Debit card loss can grow the longer you wait to report. Source: [FTC: Lost or stolen credit, ATM, and debit cards](https://consumer.ftc.gov/articles/lost-or-stolen-credit-atm-and-debit-cards)
- [ ] **mn08** (both) Myth: Wait to see if a lost card gets used. Fix: Report it right away. If you report before it is used, you owe nothing. Source: [FTC: Lost or stolen credit, ATM, and debit cards](https://consumer.ftc.gov/articles/lost-or-stolen-credit-atm-and-debit-cards)
- [ ] **mn09** (both) Myth: Credit repair firms can erase true late payments. Fix: No one can legally remove accurate, current info. Source: [FTC: Fixing your credit FAQs](https://consumer.ftc.gov/articles/fixing-your-credit-faqs)
- [ ] **mn10** (both) Myth: Bad marks last forever. Fix: Most negative info stays 7 years. Bankruptcy stays 10. Source: [FTC: Fixing your credit FAQs](https://consumer.ftc.gov/articles/fixing-your-credit-faqs)
- [ ] **mn11** (both) Myth: You have one credit score. Fix: You have many. Scores differ by model and by loan type. Source: [CFPB: What is a credit score?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-a-credit-score-en-315/)
- [ ] **mn12** (both) Myth: A credit freeze costs money. Fix: You can freeze and unfreeze for free at Equifax, Experian, and TransUnion. Source: [CFPB: What is a credit freeze?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-a-credit-freeze-or-security-freeze-on-my-credit-report-en-1341/)
- [ ] **mn13** (both) Myth: A freeze locks you out too. Fix: You can still request and review your reports. Source: [CFPB: What is a credit freeze?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-a-credit-freeze-or-security-freeze-on-my-credit-report-en-1341/)
- [ ] **mn14** (both) Myth: A payday loan fee is small. Fix: A typical 2-week $15 per $100 fee is close to 400% APR. Source: [CFPB: What is a payday loan?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-a-payday-loan-en-1567/)
- [ ] **mn15** (both) Myth: No interest means no cost. Fix: Many BNPL loans have no interest, but most charge late fees. Source: [CFPB: What is a Buy Now, Pay Later loan?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-a-buy-now-pay-later-bnpl-loan-en-2119/)
- [ ] **mn16** (both) Myth: You must apply for FDIC coverage. Fix: Coverage is automatic when you open a deposit account at an FDIC-insured bank. Source: [FDIC: Financial products that are insured](https://www.fdic.gov/resources/deposit-insurance/financial-products-insured)
- [ ] **mn17** (both) Myth: Everything at a bank is FDIC insured. Fix: FDIC covers deposit accounts. Not every product a bank offers is covered. Source: [FDIC: Financial products that are insured](https://www.fdic.gov/resources/deposit-insurance/financial-products-insured)
- [ ] **mn18** (both) Myth: An extension delays the payment too. Fix: An extension gives time to file, but you must pay by the April due date. Source: [IRS: Get an extension to file your tax return](https://www.irs.gov/filing/get-an-extension-to-file-your-tax-return)
- [ ] **mn19** (both) Myth: Debt collectors can call any time. Fix: They cannot contact you before 8 AM or after 9 PM unless you agree. Source: [FTC: Debt collection FAQs](https://consumer.ftc.gov/articles/debt-collection-faqs)
- [ ] **mn20** (both) Myth: You cannot stop calls at work. Fix: They cannot call you at work if you tell them you are not allowed calls there. Source: [FTC: Debt collection FAQs](https://consumer.ftc.gov/articles/debt-collection-faqs)
- [ ] **mn21** (both) Myth: Wait it out when you fall behind. Fix: Call creditors early, before a collector gets involved. They may lower payments. Source: [FTC: How to get out of debt](https://consumer.ftc.gov/articles/how-get-out-debt)
- [ ] **mn22** (both) Myth: Ignore collectors and they go away. Fix: Talk at least once to find out more. Do not share personal financial info. Source: [FTC: How to get out of debt](https://consumer.ftc.gov/articles/how-get-out-debt)
- [ ] **mn23** (both) Myth: Income drives your credit score. Fix: Paying on time, every time has the greatest impact. Source: [CFPB: Understand your credit score](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/consumer-tools/credit-reports-and-scores/understand-your-credit-score/)
- [ ] **mn24** (both) Myth: Using most of your limit helps your score. Fix: Keep balances low. Experts advise using no more than 30% of your limit. Source: [CFPB: Understand your credit score](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/consumer-tools/credit-reports-and-scores/understand-your-credit-score/)
- [ ] **mn25** (both) Myth: Closing old cards raises your score. Fix: Do not assume it helps. Closing a card can lower your score. Source: [CFPB: Does it hurt my credit to close a credit card?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/does-it-hurt-my-credit-to-close-a-credit-card-en-1231/)
- [ ] **mn26** (both) Myth: Credit cards always charge interest. Fix: With a grace period, paying in full on time means no interest on purchases. Source: [CFPB: What is a grace period for a credit card?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-a-grace-period-for-a-credit-card-en-47/)
- [ ] **mn27** (both) Myth: Cash advances get a grace period. Fix: Interest on a cash advance usually starts on day one. Source: [CFPB: What is a grace period for a credit card?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-a-grace-period-for-a-credit-card-en-47/)
- [ ] **mn28** (both) Myth: More applications build credit faster. Fix: Only apply for credit you need. Many apps at once can look like money trouble. Source: [CFPB: Understand your credit score](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/consumer-tools/credit-reports-and-scores/understand-your-credit-score/)
- [ ] **mn29** (both) Myth: A lower monthly payment means a cheaper loan. Fix: Longer loans cost more interest. In CFPB's example, 72 months cost more than twice the interest of 36. Source: [CFPB: How do I compare auto loan offers?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/how-do-i-compare-auto-loan-offers-what-should-i-look-at-besides-the-monthly-payment-en-753/)
- [ ] **mn30** (both) Myth: Waiting a few years to invest does not matter. Fix: The earlier you start, the more compounding helps. Starting later means you must invest more. Source: [Investor.gov: Introduction to Investing](https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing)
- [ ] **mn31** (both) Myth: You never need cash anymore. Fix: Keep a small amount of cash in small bills. ATMs and cards may not work. Source: [Ready.gov: Financial preparedness](https://www.ready.gov/financial-preparedness)
- [ ] **mn32** (both) Myth: Interest only grows on what you put in. Fix: You earn interest on the interest too, so it snowballs. Source: [Investor.gov: What is compound interest?](https://www.investor.gov/additional-resources/information/youth/teachers-classroom-resources/what-compound-interest)
- [ ] **mn50** (ya) Myth: Banks can always charge debit overdraft fees. Fix: For one-time debit and ATM use, banks cannot charge an overdraft fee unless you opted in. Source: [CFPB: Bank charged me an overdraft fee](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-can-i-do-if-my-bank-charged-me-a-fee-for-overdrawing-my-account-en-1037/)
- [ ] **mn51** (ya) Myth: Saying no to overdraft stops all fees. Fix: Banks can still charge for checks and recurring electronic payments. Source: [CFPB: Bank charged me an overdraft fee](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-can-i-do-if-my-bank-charged-me-a-fee-for-overdrawing-my-account-en-1037/)
- [ ] **mn52** (ya) Myth: Student loan interest waits until graduation. Fix: Except subsidized federal loans, interest keeps adding up while in school. Source: [CFPB: How does interest accrue while I am in school?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/how-does-interest-accrue-while-i-am-in-school-en-593/)
- [ ] **mn53** (ya) Myth: Capitalized interest is forgiven. Fix: It is added to your balance, so you pay interest on interest. Source: [CFPB: How does interest accrue while I am in school?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/how-does-interest-accrue-while-i-am-in-school-en-593/)
- [ ] **mn54** (ya) Myth: Credit scores only matter for loans. Fix: Companies also use them for renting and insurance decisions. Source: [CFPB: What is a credit score?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-a-credit-score-en-315/)
- [ ] **mn55** (ya) Myth: Employers never look at credit. Fix: Some employers use credit reports when hiring. Source: [FTC: Fixing your credit FAQs](https://consumer.ftc.gov/articles/fixing-your-credit-faqs)
- [ ] **mn56** (ya) Myth: Payday lenders make sure you can repay. Fix: Payday lenders generally do not check your ability to repay. Source: [CFPB: What is a payday loan?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-a-payday-loan-en-1567/)
- [ ] **mn57** (ya) Myth: BNPL is not really a loan. Fix: BNPL is a type of installment loan. Source: [CFPB: What is a Buy Now, Pay Later loan?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-a-buy-now-pay-later-bnpl-loan-en-2119/)
